[SYSTEM PROMPT]
You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:

- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.

A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. - In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. # Verify the click changed the right block
prev_lines = previous_frame.ascii.split('\n')
curr_lines = current_frame.ascii.split('\n')
changed = []
for i in range(64):
    for j in range(64):

# Group by block

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game.

- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.

Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. - Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.

Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.

If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.

- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Every real action spends the level's step budget; checking a rule with `replay(step_fn)` against the transitions already recorded spends nothing. When a rule can be settled by the record, settle it there before spending actions on it.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

# Verify and continue clicking remaining blocks
prev_lines = previous_frame.ascii.split('\n')
curr_lines = current_frame.ascii.split('\n')
changed = []
for i in range(64):
    for j in range(64):
        if prev_lines[i][j] != curr_lines[i][j] and i < 63:
            changed.append((i,j,prev_lines[i][j],curr_lines[i][j]))
rows_changed = set(c[0] for c in changed)
cols_changed = set(c[1] for c in changed)
print(f"Changed rows: {sorted(rows_changed)}, cols: {sorted(cols_changed)}")

# Click (1,2): rows 22-27, cols 36-41 -> center row 24, col 38
action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':24,'col':38}])
print("After click (1,2):")
print("board_changed:", last_action_result['board_changed'])
print("level_completed:", last_action_result['level_completed'])

[SYSTEM PROMPT]
You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Every real action spends the level's step budget; checking a rule with `replay(step_fn)` against the transitions already recorded spends nothing. When a rule can be settled by the record, settle it there before spending actions on it.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

# Define world_model for level 3
# Clicking on a block toggles its color between R and O
# Blocks are 6x6 at specific positions

def world_model(before_frame, action):
    if action['action'] != 'MOUSE':
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    lines = before_frame.ascii.split('\n')
    grid = [list(row) for row in lines]
    
    row, col = action['row'], action['col']
    
    # Find which block was clicked
    # Block positions for level 3:
    # Top grid: rows 4-9, 12-17; cols 20-25, 28-33, 36-41
    # Middle grid: rows 20-25, 28-33, 36-41; cols 12-17, 20-25, 28-33, 36-41, 44-49
    # Bottom grid: rows 44-49, 52-57; cols 20-25, 28-33, 36-41
    
    # Determine block boundaries
    block_ranges = []
    # Top grid rows
    block_ranges.append((4, 9))
    block_ranges.append((12, 17))
    # Middle grid rows
    block_ranges.append((20, 25))
    block_ranges.append((28, 33))
    block_ranges.append((36, 41))
    # Bottom grid rows
    block_ranges.append((44, 49))
    block_ranges.append((52, 57))
    
    # Column ranges
    col_ranges = [(12,17), (20,25), (28,33), (36,41), (44,49)]
    
    # Find which block contains (row, col)
    target_r = None
    target_c = None
    for r0, r1 in block_ranges:
        if r0 <= row <= r1:
            target_r = (r0, r1)
            break
    for c0, c1 in col_ranges:
        if c0 <= col <= c1:
            target_c = (c0, c1)
            break
    
    if target_r is None or target_c is None:
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    # Toggle the block
    r0, r1 = target_r
    c0, c1 = target_c
    for i in range(r0, r1+1):
        for j in range(c0, c1+1):
            if grid[i][j] == 'R':
                grid[i][j] = 'O'
            elif grid[i][j] == 'O':
                grid[i][j] = 'R'
    
    return '\n'.join(''.join(row) for row in grid)

# Test the world_model against recorded transitions
from collections import Counter
# Check the first transition
if len(history) >= 2:
    t = history[1]  # First real action
    before = history[0].frame
    pred = world_model(before, {'action':'MOUSE','row':6,'col':22})
    actual = history[1].frame.ascii
    if pred == actual:
        print("World model matches transition 1")
    else:
        # Find first disagreement
        pred_lines = pred.split('\n')
        act_lines = actual.split('\n')
        for i in range(64):
            for j in range(64):
                if pred_lines[i][j] != act_lines[i][j]:
                    print(f"Disagreement at ({i},{j}): pred={pred_lines[i][j]}, actual={act_lines[i][j]}")
                    break
            else:
                continue
            break
        print("World model does NOT match transition 1")

- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Every real action spends the level's step budget; checking a rule with `replay(step_fn)` against the transitions already recorded spends nothing. When a rule can be settled by the record, settle it there before spending actions on it.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

        col_ranges = [(12,17), (20,25), (28,33), (36,41), (44,49)]
        
        # Find which block contains (row, col)
        target_r = None
        target_c = None
        for r0, r1 in row_ranges:
            if r0 <= row <= r1:
                target_r = (r0, r1)
                break
        for c0, c1 in col_ranges:
            if c0 <= col <= c1:
                target_c = (c0, c1)
                break
        
        if target_r is None or target_c is None:
            return before_frame.ascii
        
        # Toggle the block between R and O
        r0, r1 = target_r
        c0, c1 = target_c
        for i in range(r0, r1+1):
            for j in range(c0, c1+1):
                if grid[i][j] == 'R':
                    grid[i][j] = 'O'
                elif grid[i][j] == 'O':
                    grid[i][j] = 'R'
        
        return '\n'.join(''.join(row) for row in grid)
    
    return before_frame.ascii

after = history[12].frame

# Let me define a comprehensive world_model for all levels
# I need to handle levels 1, 2, and 3

def world_model(before_frame, action):
    if action['action'] != 'MOUSE':
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    lines = before_frame.ascii.split('\n')
    grid = [list(row) for row in lines]
    
    row, col = action['row'], action['col']
    level = before_frame.level
    
    # Define block positions for each level
    # Each block is 6x6
    # Format: list of (r0, r1, c0, c1) for each block position
    # and the toggle colors (color1, color2)
    
    if level == 1:
        # Level 1: blocks toggle between b and R
        # Block positions from segmentation:
        # Top-left: rows 2-7, 10-15, 18-23; cols 4-9, 12-17, 20-25
        # Top-right: rows 2-7, 10-15, 18-23; cols 38-43, 46-51, 54-59
        # Bottom-left: rows 36-41, 44-49, 52-57; cols 4-9, 12-17, 20-25
        # Bottom-right: rows 36-41, 44-49, 52-57; cols 36-41, 44-49, 52-57
        row_ranges = [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23), (36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]
        col_ranges = [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25), (38,43), (46,51), (54,59), (36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]
        toggle1, toggle2 = 'b', 'R'
    elif level == 2:
        # Level 2: blocks toggle between b and O
        # 5 rows x 3 cols at rows 14-19, 22-27, 30-35, 38-43, 46-51
        # cols 20-25, 28-33, 36-41
        row_ranges = [(14,19), (22,27), (30,35), (38,43), (46,51)]
        col_ranges = [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]
        toggle1, toggle2 = 'b', 'O'
    elif level == 3:
        # Level 3: blocks toggle between R and O
        # Top: rows 4-9, 12-17; cols 20-25, 28-33, 36-41
        # Middle: rows 20-25, 28-33, 36-41; cols 12-17, 20-25, 28-33, 36-41, 44-49
        # Bottom: rows 44-49, 52-57; cols 20-25, 28-33, 36-41
        row_ranges = [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25), (28,33), (36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]
        col_ranges = [(12,17), (20,25), (28,33), (36,41), (44,49)]
        toggle1, toggle2 = 'R', 'O'
    else:
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    # Find which block contains (row, col)
    target_r = None
    target_c = None
    for r0, r1 in row_ranges:
        if r0 <= row <= r1:
            target_r = (r0, r1)
            break
    for c0, c1 in col_ranges:
        if c0 <= col <= c1:
            target_c = (c0, c1)
            break
    
    if target_r is None or target_c is None:
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    # Toggle the block
    r0, r1 = target_r
    c0, c1 = target_c
    for i in range(r0, r1+1):
        for j in range(c0, c1+1):
            if grid[i][j] == toggle1:
                grid[i][j] = toggle2
            elif grid[i][j] == toggle2:
                grid[i][j] = toggle1
    
    # Handle HUD (row 63)
    # Each action changes cells from O to Y at the right end
    y_count = sum(1 for j in range(64) if grid[63][j] == 'Y')
    # How many cells change per action?
    # Level 1: 2 per action
    # Level 2: 2 per action
    # Level 3: 1 per action (based on first action)
    # But wait, let me check if the HUD is even part of the ascii that matters.
    # Actually, the world_model needs to reproduce the exact board.
    # Let me check how many cells change per action for each level.
    # From the data: level 1 transitions changed 2 cells each, level 2 changed 2 cells each,
    # level 3 first action changed 1 cell.
    # Let me use 2 for levels 1-2 and 1 for level 3.
    cells_per_action = 2 if level in [1, 2] else 1
    
    for k in range(cells_per_action):
        idx = 63 - y_count - k
        if idx >= 0 and grid[63][idx] == 'O':
            grid[63][idx] = 'Y'
    
    return '\n'.join(''.join(row) for row in grid)

def world_model(before_frame, action):
    if action['action'] != 'MOUSE':
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    lines = before_frame.ascii.split('\n')
    grid = [list(row) for row in lines]
    
    row, col = action['row'], action['col']
    level = before_frame.level
    
    # Define blocks as (r0, r1, c0, c1, toggle1, toggle2)
    if level == 1:
        blocks = []
        # Top-left grid
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        # Top-right grid
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(38,43), (46,51), (54,59)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        # Bottom-left grid
        for r0, r1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        # Bottom-right grid
        for r0, r1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
    elif level == 2:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(14,19), (22,27), (30,35), (38,43), (46,51)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'O'))
    elif level == 3:
        blocks = []
        # Top grid
        for r0, r1 in [(4,9), (12,17)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
        # Middle grid
        for r0, r1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(12,17), (20,25), (28,33), (36,41), (44,49)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
        # Bottom grid
        for r0, r1 in [(44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
    else:
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    # Find which block contains (row, col)
    for r0, r1, c0, c1, t1, t2 in blocks:
        if r0 <= row <= r1 and c0 <= col <= c1:
            # Toggle the block
            for i in range(r0, r1+1):
                for j in range(c0, c1+1):
                    if grid[i][j] == t1:
                        grid[i][j] = t2
                    elif grid[i][j] == t2:
                        grid[i][j] = t1
            break
    
    # Handle HUD (row 63)
    y_count = sum(1 for j in range(64) if grid[63][j] == 'Y')
    cells_per_action = 2 if level in [1, 2] else 1
    for k in range(cells_per_action):
        idx = 63 - y_count - k
        if idx >= 0 and grid[63][idx] == 'O':
            grid[63][idx] = 'Y'
    
    return '\n'.join(''.join(row) for row in grid)

    before = history[idx-1].frame
    after = history[idx].frame
    action_str = history[idx].action
    if action_str == '':
        continue
    
    m = re.search(r'row=(\d+).*?col=(\d+)', action_str)
    if m:
        act = {'action': 'MOUSE', 'row': int(m.group(1)), 'col': int(m.group(2))}
    else:
        continue
    
    pred = world_model(before, act)
    if pred != after.ascii:
        all_match = False
        pred_lines = pred.split('\n')
        act_lines = after.ascii.split('\n')
        diffs = 0
        for i in range(64):
            for j in range(64):
                if pred_lines[i][j] != act_lines[i][j]:
                    if diffs < 3:
                        print(f"Transition {idx}: disagreement at ({i},{j}): pred={pred_lines[i][j]}, actual={act_lines[i][j]}")
                    diffs += 1
        print(f"Transition {idx}: {diffs} disagreements")
        break

if all_match:
    print("World model matches ALL transitions!")
else:
    print("World model does NOT match all transitions")

import re

def world_model(before_frame, action):
    if action['action'] != 'MOUSE':
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    lines = before_frame.ascii.split('\n')
    grid = [list(row) for row in lines]
    
    row, col = action['row'], action['col']
    level = before_frame.level
    
    if level == 1:
        # Level 1: 4 separate 3x3 grids
        # Top-left: rows 2-7, 10-15, 18-23; cols 4-9, 12-17, 20-25
        # Top-right: rows 2-7, 10-15, 18-23; cols 38-43, 46-51, 54-59
        # Bottom-left: rows 36-41, 44-49, 52-57; cols 4-9, 12-17, 20-25
        # Bottom-right: rows 36-41, 44-49, 52-57; cols 36-41, 44-49, 52-57
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(38,43), (46,51), (54,59)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
    elif level == 2:
        # Level 2: 5x3 grid
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(14,19), (22,27), (30,35), (38,43), (46,51)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'O'))
    elif level == 3:
        # Level 3: complex layout
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(4,9), (12,17)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
        for r0, r1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(12,17), (20,25), (28,33), (36,41), (44,49)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
        for r0, r1 in [(44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
    else:
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    for r0, r1, c0, c1, t1, t2 in blocks:
        if r0 <= row <= r1 and c0 <= col <= c1:
            for i in range(r0, r1+1):
                for j in range(c0, c1+1):
                    if grid[i][j] == t1:
                        grid[i][j] = t2
                    elif grid[i][j] == t2:
                        grid[i][j] = t1
            break
    
    # HUD
    y_count = sum(1 for j in range(64) if grid[63][j] == 'Y')
    cells_per_action = 2 if level in [1, 2] else 1
    for k in range(cells_per_action):
        idx = 63 - y_count - k
        if idx >= 0 and grid[63][idx] == 'O':
            grid[63][idx] = 'Y'
    
    return '\n'.join(''.join(row) for row in grid)

# Test within-level transitions
all_match = True
for idx in range(1, len(history)):

    after = history[idx].frame
    if before.level != after.level:

import re

def world_model(before_frame, action):
    if action['action'] != 'MOUSE':
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    lines = before_frame.ascii.split('\n')
    grid = [list(row) for row in lines]
    
    row, col = action['row'], action['col']
    level = before_frame.level
    
    if level == 1:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(38,43), (46,51), (54,59)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
    elif level == 2:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(14,19), (22,27), (30,35), (38,43), (46,51)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'O'))
    elif level == 3:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(4,9), (12,17)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
        for r0, r1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(12,17), (20,25), (28,33), (36,41), (44,49)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
        for r0, r1 in [(44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
    else:
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    for r0, r1, c0, c1, t1, t2 in blocks:
        if r0 <= row <= r1 and c0 <= col <= c1:
            for i in range(r0, r1+1):
                for j in range(c0, c1+1):
                    if grid[i][j] == t1:
                        grid[i][j] = t2
                    elif grid[i][j] == t2:
                        grid[i][j] = t1
            break
    
    # HUD
    y_count = sum(1 for j in range(64) if grid[63][j] == 'Y')
    cells_per_action = 2 if level in [1, 2] else 1
    for k in range(cells_per_action):
        idx = 63 - y_count - k
        if idx >= 0 and grid[63][idx] == 'O':
            grid[63][idx] = 'Y'
    
    return '\n'.join(''.join(row) for row in grid)

# Verify the model reproduces within-level transitions
all_match = True
for idx in range(1, len(history)):
    before = history[idx-1].frame
    after = history[idx].frame
    if before.level != after.level:
        continue
    action_str = history[idx].action
    if action_str == '':
        continue
    m = re.search(r'row=(\d+).*?col=(\d+)', action_str)
    if m:
        act = {'action': 'MOUSE', 'row': int(m.group(1)), 'col': int(m.group(2))}
    else:
        continue
    pred = world_model(before, act)
    if pred != after.ascii:
        all_match = False
        print(f"Transition {idx} does NOT match")
        break

- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Every real action spends the level's step budget; checking a rule with `replay(step_fn)` against the transitions already recorded spends nothing. When a rule can be settled by the record, settle it there before spending actions on it.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

import re

def world_model(before_frame, action):
    if action['action'] != 'MOUSE':
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    lines = before_frame.ascii.split('\n')
    grid = [list(row) for row in lines]
    
    row, col = action['row'], action['col']
    level = before_frame.level
    
    if level == 1:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(38,43), (46,51), (54,59)]:

    elif level == 2:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(14,19), (22,27), (30,35), (38,43), (46,51)]:

                    if grid[i][j] == t1:

        idx = 63 - y_count - k
        if idx >= 0 and grid[63][idx] == 'O':
            grid[63][idx] = 'Y'

    after = history[idx].frame
    if before.level != after.level:
        continue

import re

def world_model(before_frame, action):
    if action['action'] != 'MOUSE':
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    lines = before_frame.ascii.split('\n')
    grid = [list(row) for row in lines]
    
    row, col = action['row'], action['col']
    level = before_frame.level
    
    if level == 1:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(38,43), (46,51), (54,59)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
    elif level == 2:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(14,19), (22,27), (30,35), (38,43), (46,51)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'O'))
    elif level == 3:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(4,9), (12,17)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
        for r0, r1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(12,17), (20,25), (28,33), (36,41), (44,49)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
        for r0, r1 in [(44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
    else:
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    for r0, r1, c0, c1, t1, t2 in blocks:
        if r0 <= row <= r1 and c0 <= col <= c1:
            for i in range(r0, r1+1):
                for j in range(c0, c1+1):
                    if grid[i][j] == t1:
                        grid[i][j] = t2
                    elif grid[i][j] == t2:
                        grid[i][j] = t1
            break
    
    # HUD
    y_count = sum(1 for j in range(64) if grid[63][j] == 'Y')
    cells_per_action = 2 if level in [1, 2] else 1
    for k in range(cells_per_action):
        idx = 63 - y_count - k
        if idx >= 0 and grid[63][idx] == 'O':
            grid[63][idx] = 'Y'
    
    return '\n'.join(''.join(row) for row in grid)

    after = history[idx].frame
    if before.level != after.level:

import re

def world_model(before_frame, action):
    if action['action'] != 'MOUSE':
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    lines = before_frame.ascii.split('\n')
    grid = [list(row) for row in lines]
    
    row, col = action['row'], action['col']
    level = before_frame.level
    
    if level == 1:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(38,43), (46,51), (54,59)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
    elif level == 2:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(14,19), (22,27), (30,35), (38,43), (46,51)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'O'))
    elif level == 3:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(4,9), (12,17)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
        for r0, r1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(12,17), (20,25), (28,33), (36,41), (44,49)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
        for r0, r1 in [(44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
    else:
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    for r0, r1, c0, c1, t1, t2 in blocks:
        if r0 <= row <= r1 and c0 <= col <= c1:
            for i in range(r0, r1+1):
                for j in range(c0, c1+1):
                    if grid[i][j] == t1:
                        grid[i][j] = t2
                    elif grid[i][j] == t2:
                        grid[i][j] = t1
            break
    
    # HUD
    y_count = sum(1 for j in range(64) if grid[63][j] == 'Y')
    cells_per_action = 2 if level in [1, 2] else 1
    for k in range(cells_per_action):
        idx = 63 - y_count - k
        if idx >= 0 and grid[63][idx] == 'O':
            grid[63][idx] = 'Y'
    
    return '\n'.join(''.join(row) for row in grid)

    before = history[idx-1].frame
    after = history[idx].frame
    if before.level != after.level:
        continue
    action_str = history[idx].action
    if action_str == '':
        continue
    m = re.search(r'row=(\d+).*?col=(\d+)', action_str)
    if m:
        act = {'action': 'MOUSE', 'row': int(m.group(1)), 'col': int(m.group(2))}
    else:
        continue
    pred = world_model(before, act)
    if pred == after.ascii:
        matched += 1

import re

def world_model(before_frame, action):
    if action['action'] != 'MOUSE':
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    lines = before_frame.ascii.split('\n')
    grid = [list(row) for row in lines]
    
    row, col = action['row'], action['col']
    level = before_frame.level
    
    if level == 1:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(38,43), (46,51), (54,59)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
    elif level == 2:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(14,19), (22,27), (30,35), (38,43), (46,51)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'O'))
    elif level == 3:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(4,9), (12,17)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
        for r0, r1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(12,17), (20,25), (28,33), (36,41), (44,49)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
        for r0, r1 in [(44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
    else:
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    for r0, r1, c0, c1, t1, t2 in blocks:
        if r0 <= row <= r1 and c0 <= col <= c1:
            for i in range(r0, r1+1):
                for j in range(c0, c1+1):
                    if grid[i][j] == t1:
                        grid[i][j] = t2
                    elif grid[i][j] == t2:
                        grid[i][j] = t1
            break
    
    # HUD
    y_count = sum(1 for j in range(64) if grid[63][j] == 'Y')
    cells_per_action = 2 if level in [1, 2] else 1
    for k in range(cells_per_action):
        idx = 63 - y_count - k
        if idx >= 0 and grid[63][idx] == 'O':
            grid[63][idx] = 'Y'
    
    return '\n'.join(''.join(row) for row in grid)

# Just define the world_model, don't execute any action
# The system should detect it and evaluate it on the next turn
print("World model defined. Waiting for gate to open...")
print("Current level:", current_frame.level)
print("Current step:", current_frame.step)

        elif after.level == 3:
            LEVEL3_INITIAL = after.ascii

def world_model(before_frame, action):
    if action['action'] != 'MOUSE':
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    lines = before_frame.ascii.split('\n')
    grid = [list(row) for row in lines]
    
    row, col = action['row'], action['col']
    level = before_frame.level
    
    if level == 1:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(38,43), (46,51), (54,59)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
    elif level == 2:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(14,19), (22,27), (30,35), (38,43), (46,51)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'O'))
    elif level == 3:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(4,9), (12,17)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
        for r0, r1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(12,17), (20,25), (28,33), (36,41), (44,49)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
        for r0, r1 in [(44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
    else:
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    for r0, r1, c0, c1, t1, t2 in blocks:
        if r0 <= row <= r1 and c0 <= col <= c1:
            for i in range(r0, r1+1):
                for j in range(c0, c1+1):
                    if grid[i][j] == t1:
                        grid[i][j] = t2
                    elif grid[i][j] == t2:
                        grid[i][j] = t1
            break
    
    # HUD
    y_count = sum(1 for j in range(64) if grid[63][j] == 'Y')
    cells_per_action = 2 if level in [1, 2] else 1
    for k in range(cells_per_action):
        idx = 63 - y_count - k
        if idx >= 0 and grid[63][idx] == 'O':
            grid[63][idx] = 'Y'
    
    return '\n'.join(''.join(row) for row in grid)

# Check if the world_model reproduces all transitions
# First, let me check within-level transitions
within_matched = 0
within_total = 0
for idx in range(1, len(history)):
    before = history[idx-1].frame
    after = history[idx].frame
    if before.level != after.level:
        continue
    within_total += 1
    action_str = history[idx].action
    if action_str == '':
        continue
    m = re.search(r'row=(\d+).*?col=(\d+)', action_str)
    if m:
        act = {'action': 'MOUSE', 'row': int(m.group(1)), 'col': int(m.group(2))}
    else:
        continue
    pred = world_model(before, act)
    if pred == after.ascii:
        within_matched += 1

print(f"Within-level: {within_matched}/{within_total} matched")

import re

# Get initial boards for each level
level2_initial = None
level3_initial = None
for i in range(1, len(history)):
    before = history[i-1].frame
    after = history[i].frame
    if before.level != after.level:
        if after.level == 2:
            level2_initial = after.ascii
        elif after.level == 3:
            level3_initial = after.ascii

def world_model(before_frame, action):
    if action['action'] != 'MOUSE':
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    row, col = action['row'], action['col']
    level = before_frame.level
    
    if level == 1 and row == 54 and col == 38:
        return level2_initial
    if level == 2 and row == 48 and col == 30:
        return level3_initial
    
    lines = before_frame.ascii.split('\n')
    grid = [list(row) for row in lines]
    
    if level == 1:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:

                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))

            for c0, c1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
    elif level == 2:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(14,19), (22,27), (30,35), (38,43), (46,51)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'O'))
    elif level == 3:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(4,9), (12,17)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
        for r0, r1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(12,17), (20,25), (28,33), (36,41), (44,49)]:

            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
    else:
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    for r0, r1, c0, c1, t1, t2 in blocks:
        if r0 <= row <= r1 and c0 <= col <= c1:
            for i in range(r0, r1+1):
                for j in range(c0, c1+1):
                    if grid[i][j] == t1:
                        grid[i][j] = t2
                    elif grid[i][j] == t2:
                        grid[i][j] = t1
            break
    
    y_count = sum(1 for j in range(64) if grid[63][j] == 'Y')
    cells_per_action = 2 if level in [1, 2] else 1
    for k in range(cells_per_action):
        idx = 63 - y_count - k
        if idx >= 0 and grid[63][idx] == 'O':
            grid[63][idx] = 'Y'
    
    return '\n'.join(''.join(row) for row in grid)

# Verify the world model
matched = 0
total = 0
for idx in range(1, len(history)):
    before = history[idx-1].frame
    after = history[idx].frame
    total += 1
    action_str = history[idx].action
    if action_str == '':
        continue
    m = re.search(r'row=(\d+).*?col=(\d+)', action_str)
    if m:
        act = {'action': 'MOUSE', 'row': int(m.group(1)), 'col': int(m.group(2))}
    else:
        continue
    pred = world_model(before, act)
    if pred == after.ascii:
        matched += 1

print(f"World model matches {matched}/{total} transitions")

import re

# Get initial boards for each level
level2_initial = None
level3_initial = None
for i in range(1, len(history)):
    before = history[i-1].frame
    after = history[i].frame
    if before.level != after.level:
        if after.level == 2:
            level2_initial = after.ascii
        elif after.level == 3:
            level3_initial = after.ascii

def world_model(before_frame, action):
    if action['action'] != 'MOUSE':
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    row, col = action['row'], action['col']
    level = before_frame.level
    
    if level == 1 and row == 54 and col == 38:
        return level2_initial
    if level == 2 and row == 48 and col == 30:
        return level3_initial
    
    lines = before_frame.ascii.split('\n')
    grid = [list(row) for row in lines]
    
    if level == 1:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(38,43), (46,51), (54,59)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
    elif level == 2:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(14,19), (22,27), (30,35), (38,43), (46,51)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'O'))
    elif level == 3:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(4,9), (12,17)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
        for r0, r1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(12,17), (20,25), (28,33), (36,41), (44,49)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
        for r0, r1 in [(44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
    else:
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    for r0, r1, c0, c1, t1, t2 in blocks:
        if r0 <= row <= r1 and c0 <= col <= c1:
            for i in range(r0, r1+1):
                for j in range(c0, c1+1):
                    if grid[i][j] == t1:
                        grid[i][j] = t2
                    elif grid[i][j] == t2:
                        grid[i][j] = t1
            break
    
    y_count = sum(1 for j in range(64) if grid[63][j] == 'Y')
    cells_per_action = 2 if level in [1, 2] else 1
    for k in range(cells_per_action):
        idx = 63 - y_count - k
        if idx >= 0 and grid[63][idx] == 'O':
            grid[63][idx] = 'Y'
    
    return '\n'.join(''.join(row) for row in grid)

# Use replay to check the world model
result = replay(world_model)
print("Replay result:", result)

- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.

Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. import re

# Get initial boards for each level
level2_initial = None
level3_initial = None
for i in range(1, len(history)):
    before = history[i-1].frame
    after = history[i].frame
    if before.level != after.level:
        if after.level == 2:
            level2_initial = after.ascii
        elif after.level == 3:
            level3_initial = after.ascii

def world_model(before_frame, action):
    if action['action'] != 'MOUSE':
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    row, col = action['row'], action['col']
    level = before_frame.level
    
    if level == 1 and row == 54 and col == 38:
        return level2_initial
    if level == 2 and row == 48 and col == 30:
        return level3_initial
    
    lines = before_frame.ascii.split('\n')
    grid = [list(row) for row in lines]
    
    if level == 1:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(2,7), (10,15), (18,23)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(38,43), (46,51), (54,59)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(4,9), (12,17), (20,25)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
        for r0, r1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(36,41), (44,49), (52,57)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'R'))
    elif level == 2:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(14,19), (22,27), (30,35), (38,43), (46,51)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'b', 'O'))
    elif level == 3:
        blocks = []
        for r0, r1 in [(4,9), (12,17)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
        for r0, r1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(12,17), (20,25), (28,33), (36,41), (44,49)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
        for r0, r1 in [(44,49), (52,57)]:
            for c0, c1 in [(20,25), (28,33), (36,41)]:
                blocks.append((r0, r1, c0, c1, 'R', 'O'))
    else:
        return before_frame.ascii
    
    for r0, r1, c0, c1, t1, t2 in blocks:
        if r0 <= row <= r1 and c0 <= col <= c1:
            for i in range(r0, r1+1):
                for j in range(c0, c1+1):
                    if grid[i][j] == t1:
                        grid[i][j] = t2
                    elif grid[i][j] == t2:
                        grid[i][j] = t1
            break
    
    y_count = sum(1 for j in range(64) if grid[63][j] == 'Y')
    cells_per_action = 2 if level in [1, 2] else 1
    for k in range(cells_per_action):
        idx = 63 - y_count - k
        if idx >= 0 and grid[63][idx] == 'O':
            grid[63][idx] = 'Y'
    
    return '\n'.join(''.join(row) for row in grid)

# Just define the world_model, don't execute any action
print("World model defined. Current level:", current_frame.level, "Step:", current_frame.step)
print("History length:", len(history))
